When she finally glanced over her shoulder and saw me trailing her, that one-sided smile came back.
“Oh good,” she said. “You’re the curious type. Those survive longer if they pick their adults carefully.”
“I’m not—” I started, then gave up. “Where are you going?”
“Around the long edge. I hate announcing myself at front gates unless somebody’s paying me to be polite.” Her eyes flicked to me. “You can come if you keep up. Deal, snowflake?”
“…Deal.”
We talked more than I meant to.
She asked if the frost came with a headache. I said only when people stared. She asked if Haruto-san still dressed like a closing argument. I said he wore black every day. She laughed, quick and bright, and the sound made the path feel less like trespass.
The more we walked, the clearer she got.
Kei-senpai’s hair wasn’t only tied back—it was dark with a faint uneven sheen, the kind that looked different every time the light shifted through the trees. A few strands kept escaping near her cheek and she ignored them like they were beneath negotiation. Her eyes were sharp, lively, always half a step ahead of her mouth. When she listened, she listened with her whole face, not just the polite part. There was a thin old nick at the edge of her left brow, pale against her skin, and a dark cord bracelet on her wrist worn shiny in the middle from use.
She was rangy rather than delicate. Tall enough that I had to tip my chin when she turned, not taller than Haruto-san, still taller than Mother, and yes, that still impressed me. Her coat had been mended at one shoulder with thread that didn’t quite match. Her boots had seen real distance. She smelled faintly of rain-dust and metal polish, not perfume.
“You’re staring,” Kei-senpai said.
“I’m not.”
“You are. It’s fine. I am objectively interesting.”
My face heated. “You’re… different from people in the compound.”
“That’s the nicest way anyone’s called me a problem in weeks.” She nudged a loose stone off the path with her toe. “So. Cryokinesis in a neutralization clan. Public awakening. Telepath boss in black. You have a busy week for a twelve-year-old.”
“You know a lot.”
“I hear a lot.” Her smile thinned into something more serious for one breath, then bounced back. “Also, your wards gossip like aunties.”
We turned along the outer boundary where the compound’s roof ridges showed between the trees. I told her about Elder Saito’s lens. About Mei and the snow flower. About how neutralization was supposed to be our whole story and my hands had interrupted the brochure. Kei-senpai didn’t once look at me like a defect. She looked at me like a new technique she wanted to see land clean.
“Good,” she said when I finished. “Hate when clans act like the bloodline table is scripture.”
“Are you a speed type?” I asked, because something about the way she moved kept suggesting the air was optional.
Her grin went wide and dangerous in a friendly way.
“Among other things.”
Before I could ask what that meant, she stepped ahead, then blurred—
—and was suddenly circling me in a rush of displaced wind, coat snapping, boots kissing the dirt at angles that shouldn’t have been funny and were. One loop. Two. Fast enough that my hair lifted. Fast enough that my thoughts tripped over themselves.
She stopped right where she had started, not even out of breath.
“There,” Kei-senpai said. “Now you’ve been properly teased by your senpai.”
I opened my mouth, closed it, and somehow still followed when she started walking again.
Outside the compound, with a tall girl in a mended coat making the road feel like a conversation instead of a rule, winter in my blood didn’t feel like a scandal for a minute.
It just felt like something I might learn to aim.
We had followed the outer path far enough that the compound roofs looked smaller between the trees when my manners ran out of places to hide.
“How old are you?” I asked.
Kei-senpai stopped so fast I almost walked into her coat.
She turned, one brow up, mouth already shaping trouble. “Never ask a woman her age.”
I wilted a little. “Sorry—”
“Glad I’m not just ‘a woman,’ fine.”
Then, for no reason except that she could, she posed: weight on one hip, chin tipped, hand presenting her own face like a prize on a festival stall. The escaped strands at her cheek moved in the breeze as if they were in on it.
“Why don’t you guess, snowflake?”
I stared at her. Tall. Not taller than Haruto-san. Taller than Mother. Mended coat, sharp eyes, speed enough to circle me into embarrassment. Older than Aya for sure. Not Mother’s generation. The number bounced around uselessly in my head.
“…Sixteen?”
Her grin went wide.
“Ouch. Flattering, insulting, or both?” She dropped the pose and started walking again. “Keep trying while we move. Wrong answers amuse me.”
I followed, ears hot, already deciding that senpai had been the safest word I had used all day.
“Seventeen?” I tried.
“Getting warmer,” she said, not slowing her pace. “At least you didn’t guess nineteen. Nineteen sounds like someone who has a tax file in Neo-Haven and an ulcer from management.”
“Eighteen then.”
Kei-senpai snapped her fingers once, a sharp, clean sound against the quiet of the woods. “Ding ding. Eighteen. Old enough to go where I want without a trailing guard, young enough that clan elders still try to hand me advice like it’s a gift.”
I looked at her boots again as they kicked through dry leaves along the embankment. Eighteen. Six years older than me, but somehow miles further away from the compound’s walls than Ryo or Aya had ever been.
“Why are you out here?” I asked, keeping my steps even with hers. “If you’re not going through the front gate.”
“Just checking the perimeter before I drop off a message for Haruto,” she said casually. She reached into her coat, pulled out a small leather-bound tube no thicker than two fingers, and flipped it into the air once, catching it without looking. “And maybe checking to see if the rumors about a little blue-white miracle in the Kurogane yard had any truth to them.”
“It wasn't a miracle,” I muttered, my shoulders rising. “Elder Saito called it mismatched.”
“Saito talks like a ledger,” Kei-senpai chuckled. “A useful ledger, but still a ledger. You didn't answer my question from earlier, snowflake. Does the cold come out when you’re mad, or when you’re scared?”
I thought about the test crystal. The way my palm had felt like it was falling through glass. The sudden, freezing panic of everyone watching.
“When I don’t think fast enough,” I admitted quietly.
Kei-senpai stopped again, but this time she didn’t pose or tease. She looked down at me, her sharp eyes taking in the posture I’d been trained to hold since I was seven—feet planted, center low, ready to absorb or interrupt someone else's force.
“That’s your clan’s problem,” she said, her voice dropping into something surprisingly steady. “They taught you to wait. To stop things. Cold isn’t a wall, Kitsuke. Cold is momentum that froze mid-stride. If you wait until you’re cornered to use it, you’ll just freeze your own feet to the dirt.”
She tapped her dark cord bracelet against her wrist with a dry clack.
“Next time it comes,” she added, turning back toward the treeline where the compound’s back boundary stones sat half-buried in moss, “don’t try to hold it in your hand. Let it run ahead of you.”
Kei-senpai started walking again toward the mossy boundary stones, message tube tapping once against her palm.
I followed, turning her advice over in my head like a hot coal I wasn’t allowed to drop. Don’t hold it. Let it run ahead of you. It sounded simple in the same way Haruto-san’s quiet orders sounded simple: fine until you tried to live inside them.
“You’re thinking so hard I can almost hear it without being the telepath,” Kei-senpai said.
“I’m not—”
She stopped at the tree line, turned, and looked at me properly. Not the teasing glance from before. A clear, deliberate once-over that started at my still-cold hands and ended at my face, like she was filing me under a heading only she understood.
Then she leaned down just enough that her words belonged to me alone.
“For the record, snowflake,” Kei-senpai murmured, cheerful as a thrown knife, “if you ever do let that ice run ahead of you… make sure I’m there to see it. I like being first in line when cute boys become problems.”
My brain emptied.
Not partially. Completely.
She straightened, flicked the leather tube up and caught it, and strolled on toward the back boundary as if she hadn’t just set a controlled detonation under my entire skull. The mended coat, the tall shoulders, the easy grin—all of it kept moving while I stood there burning from the collar up.
Cute.
Boys.
Problems.
First in line.
I had no defense ready for any of those words in that order. Aya’s insults I could field. Mei’s affection I could survive. Haruto-san’s mind-reading I could almost manage with warning. This was different. This was going to live in my head through dinner, drills, and every future attempt to fall asleep like a song stuck on the wrong line.
Kei-senpai called back without turning around.
“Coming? Or are you going to frost the path out of embarrassment?”
I walked after her on stiff legs, ears scarlet, fully aware of one terrible new truth:
Whatever the compound said about my bloodline after this, Kei Kashimo had already won a more private kind of fight—and she hadn’t even needed speed for the finishing blow.
I woke up to shouting.
Not the normal kind—not drill counts, not Mei losing a sock, not an instructor telling someone their stance looked like wet laundry. This was sharper. Several voices at once. Footsteps crossing the central yard too fast. A door somewhere slamming hard enough to make the paper screens tremble.
I sat up. The futon was cold on the empty side where Mei sometimes crawled in and got ejected. Pale morning light cut across the floorboards. My hands felt ordinary until I remembered they weren’t anymore.
Another voice outside, closer:
“—unauthorized arrival—”
“She’s already inside the boundary—”
“Find Haruto-sama—”
I was on my feet before I finished deciding to be brave. Jacket half-on, sandals wrong-footed, heart punching. For one stupid second I thought the frost had caused another public disaster while I slept. Then I heard a laugh carry across the compound—bright, unbothered, unmistakable.
Kei-senpai.
By the time I reached the engawa, half the junior wing was pretending to sweep while looking toward the main path. Near the inner gate stood the tall girl from yesterday’s road, coat still road-dusty, message tube gone, talking to two agitated clan officers like they were the ones out of place. Morning sun caught the uneven sheen in her hair. She looked refreshed by the chaos.
Ryo crossed the yard at speed. Aya was already on the family steps, arms folded, eyes narrowed in pure eldest-daughter threat assessment. Mother’s silhouette moved behind the hall entrance.
Kei-senpai spotted me, smiled like the morning had been arranged for her convenience, and lifted two fingers in a tiny wave.
“Morning, snowflake,” she called. “Your house is lively.”
The cold in my blood stirred as if it wanted to wave back.
I stood there in crooked sandals, newly awake, and understood the day had already left my control.
I went down the steps too quickly and had to grab the rail so I wouldn’t prove every elder right about unstable children.
By the time I reached the edge of the main path, the argument had formed a proper shape. Two clan officers stood between Kei-senpai and the inner court like human gate posts. One kept pointing at the boundary registry board. The other kept not quite touching his signal charm, as if unsure whether calling more people would make him look responsible or hysterical.
“She entered through the rear stones,” the taller officer said. “No escort. No prior seal at the front.”
Kei-senpai rocked on her heels, hands in her coat pockets, profoundly unimpressed. “I delivered to Haruto at the boundary yesterday. This is follow-up. Unless Kurogane hospitality expires overnight?”
Ryo arrived first from our side, shoulders square. “State your business cleanly.”
“I did,” Kei-senpai said. Then her eyes slid to me, warm with mischief. “Ask your snowflake. He walked me halfway there.”
Every nearby face turned.
My soul attempted to leave through my ears.
Aya’s voice cut from the steps. “Kitsuke. Come here.”
I went, because Aya in public was not a suggestion. Mei peeked around her waist, whispering far too audibly, “That’s the tall one.”
Mother came out at last, hair pinned, expression locked into formal calm. She took in Kei-senpai from boot to tied-back hair in one measured pass. “You are on Kurogane ground. Names first.”
Kei-senpai’s posture eased by a degree, not submission so much as recognition of a real adult. “Kei Kashimo. Courier-adjacent. Perimeter check and secondary notice for Haruto Yagami. I’ll stand where you put me if someone feeds me tea and stops looking like I came to steal the children.”
“That remains to be determined,” Mother said.
The air changed before anyone else spoke.
Haruto-san crossed from the west corridor in black, unhurried, and the officers’ voices died as if cut clean. He did not look surprised to see Kei-senpai in the yard. He looked like a man collecting a consequence he had already budgeted for.
“Kei,” he said.
“Haruto.” She tossed him a two-finger salute. “Your gate staff are athletic this morning.”
“They are doing their jobs.” He stopped between her and the family wing without making a wall of himself. “Secondary notice?”
Kei-senpai pulled a thin folded strip from an inner pocket and held it out. “After the tube. Same sender branch. They want confirmation the lattice arrived intact and that your local freeze event was not a boundary rupture.” Her mouth tilted. “I confirmed the freeze event is approximately one twelve-year-old.”
I wanted the ground to open.
Haruto-san took the strip, scanned it once, and slipped it away. “The lattice is intact. The awakening is under evaluation. You could have sent that by sealed mark.”
“I could have,” Kei-senpai agreed. “But then I wouldn’t get to see if the snowflake survived the night.”
Aya made a noise like a tea kettle deciding on violence. Ryo’s stare could have broken bricks. Mei waved, traitorously delighted. Mother’s hand settled on my shoulder, firm and claiming.
Haruto-san glanced at me, then back to Kei-senpai. “You will not turn my junior disciples into street nicknames in the open yard.”
“Too late,” she said cheerfully. “Also, he follows interesting people. You may want to train that.”
“I am aware.”
For a moment the compound held still around the four points of the morning: Mother’s grip, Haruto’s black calm, Kei-senpai’s bright disruption, and me in the middle trying not to frost the path out of sheer social death.
Haruto-san turned slightly toward Mother. “She is here under my acceptance for now. Controlled visitor status. No further roaming.”
Mother did not look pleased. She did look practical. “Then she can explain herself indoors, not in front of half the junior wing.”
Kei-senpai brightened as if offered dessert. “Indoors works. I like ceilings.”
As the officers finally stood down, she passed close enough that only I heard her add:
“Nice sandals, snowflake. Very heroic.”
I looked down.
One sandal was on backward.
The cold in my hands flickered, useless and mortifying, while the compound morning rearranged itself around a girl who did not ask permission to become part of the day.
Mother put Kei-senpai at the low table like a polite ambush.
Tea first. Cushions second. Questions third. That was the order of Hanae Kurogane, and even a girl who could circle a courtyard before most people finished a blink seemed to understand she had entered a different kind of arena. Haruto-san sat to Mother’s right in black, posture exact. Ryo stood near the door until Mother told him to sit. Aya sat opposite Kei-senpai with the expression of someone preparing a full legal case. Mei was only present because she had made herself small enough that no one had successfully ejected her. I was parked beside Mother, one sandal corrected, soul still in exile.
Kei-senpai accepted the tea with both hands, which somehow made her look more dangerous.
“Thank you,” she said. “Nice cups. Very ‘we can be civil while deciding if you’re a problem.’”
Mother did not smile. “Why are you in my compound?”
“Secondary notice for Haruto. Boundary confirmation. Freeze event verification.” Kei-senpai blew once across the tea. “Also curiosity. The wards were practically vibrating with snowflake news.”
Aya’s eyes snapped to me. I stared into my cup hard enough to invent a new meditation style.
Haruto-san set his own cup down untouched. “She has visitor status under my acceptance. Temporary.”
“Temporary,” Mother repeated, tasting the word for poison. “Kashimo. That name travels.”
Kei-senpai’s mouth tipped. “Depends which one. If you mean the smug road menace with the fake manners and the real rescue complex, yes. Uncle Kenshi sends his regards by existing loudly in the world.”
I blinked. Uncle.
Haruto-san’s expression changed by a fraction—not surprise, recognition. Something almost like reluctant fondness moved through it and vanished.
“Of course he does,” Haruto-san said. “Under all the smugness, Kenshi is unconditionally caring, smart, and unbound. That combination creates weather everywhere he has relatives.”
Kei-senpai pointed at him with two fingers, delighted. “See? You do like him.”
“I didn’t say like.”
“You said it in the expensive way.”
Ryo frowned. “So you’re here as family business?”
“I’m here as me,” Kei-senpai said. “Family is just why people assume I can survive bad rooms.” Her gaze slid to me again, softer and worse. “Your boy already survived one public awakening and one backward sandal. He’s doing fine.”
Mei whispered, “Snowflake,” to herself like a treasure.
Aya kicked my ankle under the table without looking away from Kei-senpai. “Did you invite her onto the outer path?”
“I didn’t invite—” My voice cracked. “She was already on the road. I followed. A little.”
“A lot,” Kei-senpai corrected helpfully. “He talks when he forgets to be scared. It’s charming.”
Mother’s hand found the back of my neck, not punishing, just present. “Haruto-sama. How long does temporary last?”
Haruto-san glanced once at the folded notice strip, then at Kei-senpai, then at me. I had the horrible thought that he could hear every version of cute boys become problems still looping in my skull like a cursed training chant.
“Until the lattice is set and the boundary questions stop arriving by human courier,” he said. “She stays where assigned. No more collecting juniors on the road.”
Kei-senpai sighed as if deprived of a hobby. “Spoilsport.”
“Guest rules,” Mother said. “You will not rename my children in the yard. You will not enter the family wing unasked. You will not treat clan panic as entertainment.”
Kei-senpai held her stare, then nodded once, serious for a clean second. “Fair.” The grin returned immediately. “I’ll treat it as exclusive entertainment.”
Aya looked ready to vault the table.
Haruto-san rose first, black coat settling around him. “Enough. Kei, with me after tea. The officers need a formal mark so they stop inventing invasions. Kitsuke—”
I flinched.
“—finish your tea. You are not on trial.”
That should have helped.
It did not, because Kei-senpai mouthed snowflake at me over the rim of her cup, and Mother saw it, and Aya saw it, and Mei waved both hands like the nickname had been ratified by heaven.
Indoors, with tea steaming between us, the compound had not gotten quieter.
It had only moved the commotion to a table with better manners.
Haruto-san did not waste the tea’s aftertaste.
“Kei. With me.” Then, to Mother: “I am taking him as far as the west boundary line. Evaluation, not adventure.”
Mother’s look said she would count the minutes. Aya’s look said I would be interviewed later like a crime scene. Mei waved us off as if we were boarding a festival float.
That was how I ended up trailing Haruto-san and Kei-senpai across the compound with the cloth-wrapped lattice under Haruto-san’s arm and half the junior wing pretending not to watch us leave.
The west boundary stones sat half in moss, half in old authority. Up close they looked ordinary until you noticed the faint silver scoring in their faces—Kurogane neutralization marks layered over older cuts no one living claimed. Haruto-san knelt, set the parcel down, and folded the cloth back.
The stabilizer lattice caught the daylight and made it look colder.
Kei-senpai crouched opposite him without being asked, elbows on her knees. “Ugly little saint.”
“It is not meant to be admired,” Haruto-san said.
“Good. I’d worry if you started collecting pretty machines.” She tilted her head at the boundary stone. “You seating it to the old line or overwriting?”
“Seating.” His fingers moved along the frame’s etched channels. “If I overwrite, the elders will call it foreign rule. If I seat it, they can pretend it is reinforcement.”
“Politics with hardware.” Kei-senpai glanced at me. “Pay attention, snowflake. This is how adults keep children from becoming committee agenda.”
I stood where Haruto-san had pointed, close enough to see, far enough not to sneeze on destiny. “Will it hurt the stones?”
“No,” Haruto-san said. “It will argue with them, then settle.”
He set the lattice against the central boundary marker. For a moment nothing happened except my pulse in my tongue. Then the crystal seams woke—not violet like the travel coin, not clan-silver either. A deep, tempered glow ran outward through the mossy cuts in the stone, finding old routes like water finding channels under floorboards.
The air pressure changed.
My frost reacted on instinct, prickling under my skin. Kei-senpai’s hand came out, not touching me, just held low in warning. “Easy. Don’t answer it. Let it pass.”
I locked my knees and tried to do what she had said on the road: not clutch the cold. Not make a wall of myself. The surge in the boundary line rose, checked, and flattened into a low steady hum I felt more in my teeth than my ears.
Haruto-san’s shoulders eased a fraction. “Primary seat holds.”
Kei-senpai leaned in, eyes bright with professional interest now rather than mischief. “Clean join. Your Neo-Haven contact does good work when she isn’t emotionally weaponizing high school rejections.”
Haruto-san did not rise to it. He moved to the second stone, then the third, adjusting the lattice’s orientation by degrees only a person who listened to systems would bother with. At the fourth stone the hum stuttered. Cold flared hard in my palms hard enough that grit frosted at my feet.
“Kitsuke,” Haruto-san said, calm as drills. “Name what you feel.”
“Like the line pulled,” I said through my teeth. “And my ice wanted to pull back.”
“Good report.” He shifted the frame one notch. The stutter died. The frost at my feet stopped spreading. “The lattice is not caging you. It is keeping your first panic from ringing the whole compound like a bell.”
Kei-senpai whistled once, soft. “That’s almost tender, coming from you.”
“It is practical.”
“Sure.” She stood, brushed moss from her knee, and looked down the boundary line as the tempered glow sank into invisibility, work vanishing under ordinary stone. “You know the elders are still going to hate this.”
“Yes.”
“You doing it anyway?”
“Yes.”
I watched the two of them against the tree line—black coat and mended road coat, quiet authority and kinetic intrusion—and understood that the lattice was not only a machine. It was Haruto-san choosing prevention over pride, with a Kashimo as witness.
Kei-senpai bumped my shoulder with the back of her hand. “When this thing is set, we try your ice the right way. Not for the yard. For you.”
Haruto-san rose with the now-dim lattice seated into the boundary architecture, cloth folded under it like a closed argument. “If she trains you, she does it on my mark and inside the line.”
Kei-senpai smiled, all teeth and promise. “Look at that. Guest rules and a lesson plan.”
On the walk back, the compound looked unchanged.
Only the air at the west edge felt different—steadier, as if someone had put a hand on the house’s spine and told it not to flinch the next time a twelve-year-old learned what he was.
They got me in the family-wing hallway before I reached our door.
Ryo came from the front, Aya from the side room, and suddenly the corridor had no exits that would not require explaining myself to more people. Ryo’s arms folded. Aya blocked the light like a slender disaster.
“Talk,” Aya said.
“About which part?” I asked, which was already a mistake.
“The tall one,” Ryo said. “The road. The nickname. The part where half the compound watched you walk out with Haruto-sama and a stranger after public winter happened in the yard.”
Aya pointed at my chest. “And the part where she calls you snowflake like she paid for naming rights.”
My ears went hot on contact. “It’s just a nickname.”
“No nickname that lands that easily is ‘just’ anything,” Aya said. “Did she make you follow her?”
“No.”
“Did you follow her anyway?”
“…Yes.”
Ryo shut his eyes for a second, the long-suffering expression of a brother who had aged five years in two days. “Kitsuke. You left the compound.”
“Only the outer path.”
“That is still leaving.”
“I needed air.”
“Then breathe in the courtyard,” Aya snapped, then lowered her voice when a servant passed the far junction. “Do you like her?”
I made a noise no language wanted. “She’s—she’s senpai-shaped. She talks fast. She knows Haruto-san. She said Uncle Kenshi. That’s all.”
Aya’s eyes narrowed. “That is not all. Your face is doing the festival-lantern thing.”
“It is not.”
“It is,” Ryo said, traitorous and mild. “You’ve been pink since tea.”
I stared at the floorboards and tried to invent death. “She said if I ever let the ice run ahead of me, she wants to see it. And that she likes being first in line when cute boys become problems.”
Silence.
Then Aya whispered, with pure fury, “I’m going to fold her into a storage bin.”
Ryo dragged a hand down his face. “You can’t fold Haruto-sama’s accepted visitor into a storage bin.”
“I can try.”
“Please don’t.” I looked up, miserable. “She’s not… cruel. She made the officers angry, not us. Haruto-san knows her people. He likes Kenshi under all the smugness. I don’t think she’s here to hurt the house.”
Aya stepped closer, and for a moment the sharpness softened into something harder to defend against: worry. “People can like you and still pull you out of your depth. You’re twelve. You awakened in front of the whole clan. You do not need a road girl turning you into her favorite emergency.”
Ryo’s hand landed on my shoulder, heavy and warm. “We’re not mad that you froze the crystal. We’re mad that the world is arriving through every gate at once and you keep standing in the middle of it.”
I swallowed. “Haruto-san seated the lattice. The line feels steadier. Kei-senpai wants to train the ice the right way, on his mark.”
Aya and Ryo exchanged a look over my head, the private older-sibling language I was never meant to read fluently.
“Then training happens where we can see the door,” Aya said at last. “And if she calls you snowflake in the yard again, I’m answering for you.”
“Please don’t.”
“I’m answering,” she repeated.
Ryo squeezed once and let go. “Next time you leave the walls, you tell me. Not after. Before.”
“Yes.”
They released the corridor. Aya went first, already muttering about tall visitors and nickname laws. Ryo waited until I moved, then followed like a rear guard.
I stood one breath longer in the hallway with frost prickling faintly in my fingers and Kei-senpai’s voice still lodged under my ribs.
Cute boys become problems.
Family, it turned out, became problems even faster.
The next time I recalled the name Kenshi Kashimo, it was not from Kei-senpai’s mouth.
It came up from an older memory, the kind that sits under floorboards until somebody steps on the right board. I had been eight or nine, trailing behind a stack of assessment ledgers in Elder Saito’s corridor while he complained to no one in particular about outside talents that refused to fit clan columns. Most days Elder Saito spoke about abilities like weather reports: probabilities, risks, recommended containment. Careful. Dry. Softened for whoever paid for the diagnosis.
He had not softened Kenshi Kashimo.
“That one,” he had said, tapping a closed file with the back of a knuckle, “is not a technique problem. He is a personality that happens to move too fast. Smug on purpose. Useful against his will. If he likes you, he will carry your disaster farther than policy recommends. If he does not, he will still joke on the way past it. Do not confuse either behavior for simplicity.”
Someone had asked whether Kashimo was dangerous.
Elder Saito had thought for a longer time than he gave most patients.
“Yes,” he said. “And no. He is unbound. That is rarer than malice and harder to chart.”
I had not understood the word then the way I did now.
Standing in the family-wing hallway after Aya and Ryo released me, with Kei-senpai’s uncle-name still fresh in the house, the old sentence finally fit its shape. Elder Saito could dress a bloodline mismatch in gentle paperwork when he wanted. He had not bothered with Kenshi. He had spoken about him like a known storm route: not evil, not safe, impossible to file under ordinary obedience.
Which meant Kei-senpai had not arrived out of nowhere.
She had arrived out of weather the compound already half-remembered.
I found Haruto-san in the side study after the boundary hum had settled into the stones.
He was resealing the lattice cloth with neat, unhurried pulls, black sleeves precise against the low light. For once there was no elder in the room and no officer waiting for a mark. Just the quiet of a man finishing work he expected people to argue about later.
I stopped in the doorway long enough to lose courage, then stepped in anyway.
“Haruto-san.”
He did not look surprised. “You want the version that is not a nickname and not a ledger.”
My mouth opened, shut, then accepted that shoji screens and timing were both useless. “Kenshi Kashimo. Kei-senpai said uncle. Elder Saito talked about him when I was little. You… like him.”
Haruto-san tied the last corner and rested his hands on the parcel. “Sit.”
I sat on the edge of the guest cushion, back too straight.
“Kenshi is Neo-Haven weather,” Haruto-san said. “Speed first. Mouth second. Heart in both, whether he admits it or not. He will call strangers darling, walk into other people’s disasters, and look offended if you call that kindness.” A faint almost-smile touched his face and left. “Under the smugness he is unconditionally caring, smart, and unbound. That combination makes institutions nervous and survivors loyal.”
“Is he dangerous?”
“Yes.” Haruto-san met my eyes. “So is every useful person in a bad age. Kenshi’s danger is not that he hunts children or clans. It is that he refuses small cages and teaches others, by proximity, that refusal is possible.”
I thought of Kei-senpai circling me on the outer path, of Elder Saito saying unbound like a diagnosis he respected against his will. “Why does Kei-senpai carry messages for you?”
“Because Kashimo routes reach places Kurogane banners do not, and because Kenshi’s people have a habit of arriving before official channels finish pretending nothing is wrong.” Haruto-san tilted his head slightly. “Also because Kei enjoys testing doors. Including mine.”
My ears warmed. “Should I be careful around her?”
“Yes.” No decoration on the word. Then, quieter: “Careful is not the same as ashamed. She saw your ice and did not call it contamination. That matters. Do not hand her your whole balance in return for one good look.”
I nodded, fingers curled tight on my knees. “Elder Saito was more honest about Kenshi than he is about most diagnoses.”
“Saito prefers chartable gifts,” Haruto-san said. “Kenshi is not chartable. He is what happens when talent keeps its sense of humor and its loyalties outside the registry.” He paused. “If you train with Kei, remember that. Kashimo affection is real. It is also fast. You are allowed to learn from it without being carried off by it.”
The lattice sat between us under cloth, steady as the line he had forced into the stones.
“Thank you,” I said.
Haruto-san returned to the parcel, voice mild again. “If she calls you snowflake in the yard, your sister will declare war. Try not to enjoy that too visibly.”
I left the study with Kenshi Kashimo no longer just a rumor in a childhood corridor.
He was a shape Haruto-san respected: smug, unbound, and caring hard enough to make careful men account for the weather.
In the dream the compound had no doors.
Only white ground and a sky the color of old iron, and something tall enough to make the banners look like toys. It was made of ice, but not the clean kind from Mei’s flower. This ice had joints. A head like a broken shrine gate. When it moved, the air screamed sideways.
I ran. My feet punched through crust and came up numb. The thing’s shadow caught me anyway, arm descending like a falling roof. Cold poured over my shoulders with the weight of a verdict.
I tried to answer with my own frost and felt it laugh without a mouth.
The white came up to my throat—
—and the world tore left.
Six dark-bright wings cut the iron sky, not feathered in any bird way I knew, a violence of violet-edged shelter. At the same time a streak of blue plasma hooked around the giant’s wrist and wrenched, loud as a split rail. Heat and impossible speed stitched the air into burning lines. The ice creature staggered. Sheets of it sheared off and became hard rain.
I fell hard, palms stinging, and looked up.
Two figures stood between me and the ruin.
One held the winged dark like a mantle that obeyed him, posture tired and exact, as if monsters were another kind of paperwork that still had to be finished. The other one bounced once on the balls of his feet in the afterburn of blue light, silver flash in his hair, grin already halfway to a joke.
They turned toward me.
I did not know their names. I only knew the wings made the giant hesitate, and the blue plasma still crawled in little loyal sparks around the second man’s hands, and both of them looked at me too carefully for strangers.
The grinning one tipped his head, eyes bright with rude affection.
“So this little guy is your plan, Nao-chan?”
The winged man’s answer never arrived.
The iron sky cracked down the middle, the giant froze mid-collapse, and the whole dream dropped out from under me like a pulled board—
I woke in our room with my breath white in ordinary dark, hands locked in frost to the edge of the futon, and the shape of those wings still burned behind my eyes.
I did not tell anyone.
Not Mother. Not Ryo. Not Aya. Not Haruto-san, even though the winged man in the dream had stood with the same tired exactness. Not Kei-senpai, even though the blue-plasma grin had worn her uncle’s weather. The dream was mine. So was the problem inside it.
I went to the west boundary before the drill bells.
The lattice under the stones made the air feel braced, like a held note. Moss. Old cuts in the markers. No audience except a crow that decided I was not worth the branch it already had. I put my sandals true this time, planted my feet the way Ryo had hammered into me since I was old enough to hold a posture, and brought the dream back on purpose.
Giant ice. Jointed. Shrine-gate head. Cold with a verdict in it.
My pulse jumped. Frost answered too fast, spraying ugly white across the dirt.
“No,” I muttered, and hated how young the word sounded in the open air. I shook the panic out of my fingers and tried again the way Kei-senpai had said on the road— not a wall, not a clutch. Momentum frozen mid-stride. Let it run ahead.
I threw the cold forward instead of holding it in my palm.
A line of ice cracked out from my heel in a clean vein, low and mean, and stopped when I cut the breath that fed it. Not a flower. Not a yard accident. A cut.
The dream-giant still towered in my head. I narrowed it. Smaller. Mine. I forced the shape down until it was only a weight in my wrists and a pressure behind my teeth, then pushed again. A second line crossed the first. A third failed and spidered. I reset. Again. Again, until the failure made me angry instead of scared.
Anger was better.
Anger aimed.
By the fourth try the frost went where I pointed and died where I told it to die. My hands shook. My sleeves were stiff with rime. Sweat cooled too fast on my back. No wings arrived. No blue plasma joke saved the path. Just me and the boundary hum and a weapon that still only half obeyed.
I stood there until my breathing evened.
“If that thing is mine,” I told the empty line, quiet enough that it didn’t become theater, “I’m not waiting for anyone next time.”
I scrubbed the worst of the ice into the moss with my sandal, left the lattice stones looking almost undisturbed, and walked back before the compound finished waking.
They could keep their charts and nicknames.
I had work.
The challenge came at midday drills, which meant everybody who mattered was already arranged to watch.
Instructor Daisuke called pairs for control tests. Most were age-matched. Mine was not.
“Kitsuke Kurogane. Against Toma.”
A murmur went through the lines like wind under paper.
Toma was fifteen, tall for it, neutralization already clean enough that instructors used him to humble loud juniors. He stepped into the center square with the calm of someone who expected the morning to stay simple. His eyes flicked once to the frost-scar still faintly ghosting the old demonstration post, then to me.
“I’ll keep it light,” he said, not unkind. Just certain.
I bowed because the form required it. Around the yard: Ryo’s jaw set hard at the edge of the intermediate line. Aya’s arms folded so tight her sleeves creased. Mei on the bench with both hands over her mouth. Mother under the east eave, still as a held blade. Haruto-san on the raised path in black, expression unreadable. Kei-senpai leaning against a support post like the compound had installed her there for commentary, eyes bright.
“Begin on contact,” Daisuke said. “No maiming. First clean dominance ends it.”
Toma came in straight—silver neutralization blooming around his forearms, the clan answer, interruption made polite and heavy. He meant to catch my wrists, blunt the cold before it started, and end the embarrassment early.
I did not wait for the wall.
I let the ice run ahead.
Frost cracked out from my front foot in a low hard line and took the ground under his lead step. Not a flower. Not a panic burst. A cut. Toma’s balance hitched half a breath—only half—but it was enough for the yard to hear the change in the air. His neutralization flared brighter, slamming down to kill the sheet.
Silver met white with a sound like glass under a boot.
Cold climbed my arms in a clean rush. I threw the second line wider, not at his chest, at the circle around him, forcing him to spend power on the floor while I moved. He was older. Stronger. Better trained in the clan’s real language. Every time his interruption touched my frost it died in ugly steam. Every time I stopped clutching and pushed, new ice took the space he had just cleared.
“Enough playing,” Toma muttered, and the silver came on proper—full junior-elite pressure, the kind that made younger disciples’ knees dip when it passed them.
It hit my shoulder and the cold in me staggered. For one bad second the dream-giant raised its shrine-gate head behind my eyes. I almost grabbed. Almost made a wall of myself and froze my own feet to the dirt.
Let it run ahead.
I exhaled hard and sent the frost out through the contact instead of against it. Rime exploded along his neutralization sleeve in a glittering shell. Not deep. Not victory. Enough that Toma had to break stance to shatter it, and in that break I drove a third line under his heel.
He went down to one knee.
The yard made a single collective sound.
Toma’s face changed—surprise first, then heat. He surged up with a broad silver pulse meant to erase the whole square. The lattice in the west stones hummed at the edge of hearing, taking the worst of the ring before it became another public incident. My ice met the pulse, cracked, held in jagged patches, and for a moment we were just two bloodlines arguing in the open about which one got to define the morning.
“Hold!” Daisuke’s voice cut across us. “Hold—dominance recognized. Kurogane advance.”
Toma stayed on one knee a second longer than pride wanted, breath rough, staring at the frost still smoking around his sandal. Then he bowed from there, sharp and correct.
“I underestimated you.”
I bowed back because my arms were shaking and form was the only adult thing I still had ready. “You are still stronger.”
“Today didn’t care,” he said, almost smiling, almost not.
Only then did I hear the rest of the compound again—whispers, one sharp laugh from Kei-senpai’s direction, Ryo’s exhale like he’d been underwater, Aya already moving, Mother’s hand tight on the eave post. On the raised path Haruto-san had not moved at all, but the stillness in his face had shifted into something closer to attention than judgment.
I stood in the center square with cold dying obediently in my palms and an older boy’s respect still hanging in the air between us.
In front of everyone.
Not a miracle.
A result.
The yard had not fully settled from Toma when another voice cut across it.
“Hold on.”
Not an instructor. One of the older intermediates stepping out of line without waiting for permission—broad-shouldered, scraped knuckles, a face built for taking hits and grinning through the receipt. No silver around his wrists. No flare of clan weave when he moved. Just weight and timing and the plain fact of a body that had learned the hard way.
Jin Kurogane.
Gimmickless. The word the compound used carefully, like it might insult the air. In a bloodline that prized interruption, Jin had never awakened anything but stubbornness. Instructors used him for form work. Disciples underestimated him once.
He stopped at the edge of the center square and bowed to Daisuke first, then to me.
“By the rules, if he just used his weave in a dominance bout, he still owes a hand-to-hand pass,” Jin said. Casual. Clear. “I’ll take it. No abilities. His or mine.”
A few people laughed under their breath—nervous, not cruel. Aya’s head snapped up. Ryo shifted like he might intervene. Haruto-san did not move from the raised path, which was its own kind of permission.
Daisuke studied Jin, then me. “Kurogane Jin proposes a sealed exchange. Pure hand-to-hand. First clean throw or yield. Kitsuke—accept or decline.”
Jin looked at me and used my name like it belonged in the same house as his.
“Kitsuke. Not trying to put you in the dirt for sport.” His mouth tilted. “Trying to show you a fight can be fun when it isn’t a trial. Adrenaline. Footwork. Breathing. The part where you stop being a problem on a chart and start being a body in a square.”
The cold in my palms wanted to answer out of habit. I locked it down. Rules were rules. And something in the way he said Kitsuke—not the freeze boy, not the mismatch—landed harder than Toma’s respect.
“I accept,” I said.
We stepped in.
No silver. No frost. Just the packed sand and the sound of the compound remembering how to watch something ordinary become important. Jin raised his hands in a practical guard, not pretty.
“On you,” he said. “Come.”
I moved first because waiting would turn it back into fear. He met me with a forearm check, light, then a sweep that almost took my ankle and didn’t, on purpose. The near-miss sent a clean jolt up my spine—heat without panic. Jin’s eyes brightened.
“There. Feel that? That’s the good kind.”
We traded. I clipped his ribs with an elbow that would have been stronger if my arms weren’t still tired from ice. He answered with a shoulder bump that spun me half around and left me standing. Not dominance. Invitation. Every exchange stayed inside the line of teaching without becoming pity. When I finally caught his sleeve and nearly hooked his knee, he laughed once, short and real, and let the square hear it.
“Good,” Jin said. “Again.”
I went again.
By the third exchange my lungs burned and my head had no room left for dream-giants or bloodline pamphlets. Only distance, weight, the scrape of sandals, and a gimmickless older boy from my own name making the fight feel like belonging instead of judgment. He took one clean opening—hip throw, controlled, enough to put my back on the sand without driving the air out of me—and pinned the count long enough for Daisuke to mark it.
“Exchange to Jin.”
Jin pulled me up by the forearm before the dust settled. Close enough that only I got the quieter part.
“Clan’s not only the ones who light up,” he said. “Some of us are on your side the boring way. Remember the fun when the other kind of fight comes back.”
He ruffled my hair once, brother-blunt, and stepped off the square like he had only borrowed it.
Around us the yard adjusted—some impressed, some thoughtful, Aya still fierce, Ryo less braced, Kei-senpai’s grin sharp with approval from the post. On the raised path Haruto-san’s gaze rested on Jin a moment longer than on most disciples, as if filing away a different kind of strength.
I stood there with sand on my back and adrenaline singing clean in my blood, no frost required.
For the first time since the crystal turned white, someone from the compound had met me in the open and asked for nothing but a fight worth smiling through.
After midday drills, Kei-senpai did not wait for a committee.
She appeared at the edge of the junior line with two practice straps over one shoulder and a look that said the guest rules had developed a training clause. Haruto-san, from across the yard, gave a single nod. That was the whole permit.
“With me, Kitsuke,” she said. First name. Open. “West edge. Inside the line. Bring the part of you that put Toma on a knee and the part that let Jin throw you without turning into weather.”
Aya started to rise. Ryo touched her sleeve. Mother watched from the shade and did not stop it. I followed Kei-senpai past the posts to the strip of packed ground near the boundary stones, where the lattice still made the air feel braced and the compound’s eyes thinned out.
She dropped the straps in the dirt. “Official lesson. Not a flirt. Not a show. You already proved the yard can see you. Now we make the ice obey when nobody is grading it.”
“Yes, senpai.”
“Good manners. Keep them for your mother.” She pointed at the ground. “Stance like Jin forced out of you. Low. Honest. When I move, you do not freeze a wall. You send the cold ahead of my step and cut the path. If you clutch, you lose. If you panic, you lose. If you try to impress me, I’ll make you run the boundary until your nickname becomes medical.”
I set my feet. The morning still lived in my arms—Toma’s silver, Jin’s hip throw, adrenaline gone clean instead of sour. Kei-senpai blurred half a pace, not full speed, just enough to make ordinary timing feel late.
I pushed the frost forward.
A low white vein cracked out ahead of her lead foot. She hopped it, grinning, and came from the other angle. Second line. Wider. She stamped through the edge and the ice shattered wrong—my old habit, spray instead of cut.
“Again. Less spray. More intention.”
We worked until my breath scraped. She made me throw without a target, then at her shadow, then at a mark she cut in the dirt with her heel. Every time I held the cold in my palm like a shield, she slapped my wrist with two fingers and said, “Ahead.” Every time the line ran true and died where I cut it, she only said, “Yes,” and made the next one harder.
Once, the dream-giant lifted its head when she rushed me full enough to spike my pulse. Frost surged ugly up my forearms.
I forced it down the way I had at the stones before dawn. Out. Not clutch. The surge flattened into a single hard sheet that took the ground between us and stopped clean. Kei-senpai braked on it, boots hissing, and looked down at the ice with open professional pleasure.
“There,” she said. “That’s not a yard accident. That’s a tool.”
Sweat stung my eyes. Cold smoked off my sleeves. “Jin said some of the clan is on my side the boring way.”
“Jin’s right.” Kei-senpai flicked a shard off her toe. “I’m on your side the annoying way. Different service. Same outcome—you don’t get eaten by your own chart.” She stepped in, tapped my sternum once. “Hand-to-hand keeps you human. Ice keeps you alive. Use both or you’re only half a problem, and half-problems get filed.”
She tossed me a water flask like we were equals in the work if not in the years.
“Same time tomorrow unless Haruto locks me in a cupboard. Next lesson you move while the ice moves. Today you only had to make it run.”
I drank, arms shaking, and looked at the clean white cuts scarring the strip beside the boundary stones—mine, deliberate, no audience required.
For the first time, the power felt less like a public verdict and more like something I could carry on purpose.
The next morning I went up on the north storehouse roof before the bells.
I told myself it was for air. It was also because the ground still felt full of eyes. Frost practice with Kei-senpai had left clean cuts in the west strip; the yard still held Toma’s knee and Jin’s throw. Up high, the compound looked smaller. Manageable.
Jin was already there.
He sat on the ridge with one knee up, watching the first pale line come into the sky, as if roofs were a normal place to keep appointments. When the boards shifted under my sandals he didn’t startle.
“Figured,” he said. “Sunrise is when people who can’t sleep honestly come to pretend they’re training.”
I sat a little down-ridge from him. “Did you follow me?”
“Heard you on the ladder. You’re not quiet when you’re thinking.” He passed me a warm bottle from beside his hip. Barley tea, not sweet. “Drink. Your shoulders are up around your ears again.”
We sat with the tea and the cold edge of morning. Below us a few servants crossed the outer path. No drills yet. No instructors. No Kei-senpai turning the air into a lesson.
“You meant what you said in the square,” I said. “About the clan being on my side the boring way.”
Jin nodded once. “I did.”
“People call you unbeatable.”
He huffed, not quite a laugh. “People talk for sport. What they mean is I’m hard to put away clean.” He turned the bottle in his hands. “In a clan that silences gifts, I don’t have a gift to silence. You can’t shut off what isn’t there. So they get me instead—footwork, timing, stubbornness. That’s all.”
I watched our breath show faintly in the air. “Does it bother you?”
“Sometimes.” He said it plain. “Most days it’s useful. Charts don’t know where to put me, so they leave me room. Yesterday I used some of that room for you.”
“Thank you.”
“Don’t thank me like I did you a favor in court.” Jin bumped his knee against mine, light. “I wanted a real exchange. You gave me one. That’s enough.”
The sun cleared the far trees and put a thin gold line across the roof tiles. Jin stood, rolled his neck, and offered me a hand up.
“Come on, Kitsuke. Before your sister climbs up here and files a complaint against the sky.”
We went down the ladder the ordinary way. No speeches. No vows. Just a gimmickless older boy from my own name and the quiet fact that some kinds of strength only look like absence until you try to silence them.
I saw them by the east records annex.
The courier was the same cut of man from the roof warning days ago—travel coat, sealed case, the kind of quiet that made officers stand straighter. Beside him stood an elder I did not know well enough to name on sight. New to the inner rotation, or newly visible. They spoke too close. The courier’s mouth barely moved. The elder’s hand rested on the case like a blessing that was not one. When a pair of juniors passed, both men smiled at the same time, the same amount, and the smile did not reach either pair of eyes.
Weird was not the right word.
Rehearsed was closer.
I turned to find Ryo, or Haruto-san, or anyone with a rank and a spine—
—and the elder was already in my path.
I had not heard him leave the annex wall. Up close he looked kindly the way polished wood looks kindly: smooth, intentional, expensive to distrust. His smile was gentle. Real enough to make my distrust feel rude.
“Kitsuke,” he said, first name, easy as family. “A moment.”
“Elder—”
“Walk with me only as far as the post.” He did not touch me. He did not need to. The path behind me felt suddenly longer than the one ahead. “You have had a loud week. Awakening. Public bouts. Visitors. The compound stares hard when a child becomes interesting.”
My frost stirred. I held it down.
“I only wanted to ask about the courier,” I said.
“Of course you did. Bright boy.” The smile deepened a fraction. “Look out for the rest of the clan elders. Not all attention is protection. Some of it is inventory. They will smile while they decide what to do with a gift that does not match the brochure.”
Cold slid under my ribs for reasons that had nothing to do with cryokinesis.
“Whenever you feel like they will be done with you,” he continued, still gentle, still kind, “tell me. I will take you to a place better than this compound.”
He said it like shelter.
It landed like a door closing somewhere I could not see.
“I should go,” I said. “Mother expects—”
“Naturally.” He stepped aside at once, as if he had never blocked anything in his life. “No need to trouble Haruto-sama with nerves. He carries enough. This is only an open hand, Kitsuke. For later.”
He returned toward the annex, unhurried. The courier was gone. The sealed case was gone. Only the elder’s soft expression lingered in the air like perfume on a blade.
I stood on the path with my message unsaid and my pulse too loud.
He had sounded like he was on my side.
Against the stares. Against the charts. Against being finished with.
And somehow that was the part that felt worst—because if he was against the rest of the clan, he was against Haruto-san too, and men who smiled that gently while drawing a line through a whole house were not offering rescue.
They were offering a different cage.
I was crossing the inner court with a stack of cleaned straps when the air changed.
Not lattice-hum. Not drill noise. A hard snap of displaced wind near the west practice strip—Kei-senpai’s kind of sound—cut short by a sound I had never heard from her: a short, involuntary grunt of impact.
I dropped the straps and ran.
Kei-senpai was on one knee in the packed dirt, coat torn at the shoulder, a thin line of blood at the corner of her mouth. Across from her stood a woman in matte travel black, hair bound tight, posture almost polite. No clan mark I knew. No wasted movement. Kei-senpai blurred—true speed, the kind that made officers swear—and the woman was simply not where the hit landed. A palm touched Kei-senpai’s ribs. Kei folded, tried to rise, and the woman’s heel hooked her ankle with surgical neatness. Kei hit the ground hard enough that the boundary stones seemed to flinch.
“Senpai—!”
I threw the cold without permission from anyone.
Frost ripped ahead of me in a hard white vein. The woman turned her head as if noticing a spilled cup. She stepped through the edge of my line before it fully set, too fast for the cut to matter, and then she was past my guard as if distance were a suggestion.
I pivoted. Jin’s stance. Hands up. Tried to meet her the boring way—
—empty air.
Pressure bloomed at the side of my neck, precise and professional. Cold flooded my limbs for the wrong reason. My knees went first. The courtyard tilted.
Far end of the path: Mother running, pin already half-loose. Ryo behind her, full sprint. Aya with Mei dragged against her side, Mei’s mouth open around my name. Haruto-san in black cutting across the yard like a verdict that might arrive too late. Jin’s voice somewhere shouting for a hold.
They were coming.
I tried to stay upright long enough to see them reach me.
The woman’s voice found my ear anyway, soft as a closed door.
“My master is gonna love you.”
The court went white, then nothing.
End of this story.
Not the end of Kitsuke Kurogane.
They took him out of the compound cleaner than a theft and quieter than a declaration.
By the time Haruto-san reached the court, Kei Kashimo was down, the boundary lattice was humming like a struck wire, and Kitsuke’s frost was only a broken white scuff on the dirt where a twelve-year-old had tried to cut a professional’s path and learned the difference between a yard and the world. Hanae Kurogane’s voice carried farther than policy. Ryo hit the empty air where his brother had been. Aya held Mei back hard enough to hurt them both. Jin arrived gimmickless and furious, with nothing to silence and no one left to throw.
The woman was already gone.
No clan name on the wind. Only the residue of speed, a sedative measured for a child who had just become interesting, and one line left behind in a boy’s last clear second:
My master is gonna love you.
In the days after, the compound did what houses do when a child vanishes inside their own walls. It argued. It reinforced. It blamed visitors, wards, pride, timing. Haruto Yagami did not waste the noise. He tightened the line, kept Kei under roof until she could stand without lying about the pain, and put Jin where younger disciples could see that strength without a gift still counted. Hanae kept a light on in the family wing like a fact. The elders discovered that some silences were not obedience.
Somewhere beyond the banners, Kitsuke Kurogane slept under someone else’s plan—cryokinesis, stubbornness, barley-tea loyalties and all—held in reserve by a master who collected useful winters.
This story leaves him there.
On ice.
Not erased. Not finished. Only paused until the next creative thaw, when the compound, the road, or the boy himself forces the world to remember that a mismatched gift still belongs to the one who learned to make it run ahead.
